A target below the hard cap makes normal output comfortably safe, while the narrow trim prevents a pathological tail from changing the structured judgment. The rationale already existed as persisted provenance but was invisible to the person evaluating the image; showing it alongside the selected image makes the estimate inspectable without promoting it to Property truth. Preserving only implicated malformed EXTRACT fields gives the corrective call the evidence it lacked while respecting the scope contract: identical values for distinct concrete targets must not be collapsed. | §10.2, §10.8, §13, §20; `worker/src/manzil_worker/llm/`, `worker/src/manzil_worker/stages/`, `frontend/src/features/listings/`, `frontend/API_ASSUMPTIONS.md`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-07 | **v3.60: the Hunt activity union leaves the public schema; Owner and Site Admin access use separate boundaries.** Supersedes v3.40's access mechanism while preserving its live-derived contents and Owner/Site Admin visibility. The raw union is `private.hunt_activity_all`, a `security_barrier` view with no client grants. An authenticated caller can request only one Hunt through `get_hunt_activity(hunt_id, limit, before)`; the definer RPC checks exact Owner role first and caps the page at 500. Site Admin access stays in the service-role Admin API after its live `AdminUser` check and reads the private view directly. The old public `hunt_activity` view is dropped. | The v3.40 definer view was correct about why an invoker view could not serve a non-member Site Admin, but put the entire cross-tenant boundary in one public view predicate and granted raw SELECT to broad PostgREST roles. Separating the two principals removes that unnecessary common boundary: an Owner proves Hunt-local authority in a narrow RPC, while an account-level operator proves account-level authority at the API seam already responsible for audit and revocation. Keeping the union private also means a future predicate edit cannot turn a public relation into a project-wide activity export. | §4.2, §8.3, §16, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `supabase/migrations/20260901000011_hunt_activity_access.sql` |
| 2026-08-07 | **v3.59: DM-9 narrows to "submit a URL, watch it populate the Overview" — no checkpoint rendering, and the replayed Listing is staged rather than conjured.** Owner-directed scope cut. The replay no longer parks at a recorded checkpoint or shows its prompt: `checkpoint_asked`/`checkpoint_answered`/`checkpoint_auto_resolved` are treated as `completed`-shaped beats, so a capture from a Job that genuinely parked still animates straight through. `_DETAIL_KEYS` accordingly drops `prompt`, `answer` and `choice`, and the exporter no longer requires or refuses a checkpoint — any finished Job is a usable capture. Three defects that made the exporter unrunnable are fixed with it: it selected `p.locality`, a column that does not exist (the column is `city`), so **the command had never once run**; the emitted Listing carried no `floor_plans` or `sources`, which `unitGroups.ts` iterates unguarded and would have thrown on the Overview the moment the replay finished; and `stage_index` counted `started` events, which the ingest pipeline never emits, freezing `PipelineTrack` at the first phase. The bundle is now built from **named columns only** — no `select *`, which on a service-role pool returns `property_sources.cleaned_text` — and omits `added_by`. Finally, `scripts/seed_demo_hunt.py` stages the captured Listing as `archived`, so it is absent from the Overview until a visitor "submits" its URL and the replay publishes it. | Rendering a real checkpoint prompt turned out to cost far more than it was worth. The prompt text interpolates `flag.note`, which can carry a verbatim page excerpt or model-authored prose derived from the page, and `capture.json` is imported at **build time** — a world-readable static chunk, not something a demo session gates. Publishing it would have required a per-checkpoint-kind allow-list keyed to the originating flag's `check`, to buy an animation nobody asked for. Cutting it deleted four of five open security blockers on the slice at once. Staging the Listing as `archived` is the piece that makes the demo's payoff real rather than hollow: a cache-only row would render on the Overview and then dead-end, because every drawer sub-query resolves against a `hunt_listing_id` RLS has never heard of. `useListings` already filters `status = 'active'`, so `archived` hides it with no demo branch in any read path, and the row the visitor clicks is genuinely there — real score breakdown, real evidence, real Sources. The Demo Hunt therefore stays an ordinary Hunt the Owner ingests into and is billed for normally, which is what §3 has always said it is. | §3, §10, §13, §16, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `worker/.../export_demo_capture.py`, `frontend/src/features/demo/replay/`, `scripts/seed_demo_hunt.py` |
| 2026-08-07 | **v3.58: cleaned Source text is unreadable in its second home, the checkpoint RPC merges instead of replacing, and `job_events` stops storing a page prefix.** §16 control 2 claimed cleaned Source text was service-role-only "for every member"; `jobs.payload.run_state.sources[].cleaned_text` and `payload.auto_resolved_checkpoint.snapshot` are the same page bodies, `jobs` is member-selectable, and 5330 characters read back under an ordinary member's JWT. The fix is on the **read** side: a stored generated column `jobs.payload_public` — `payload` with every `cleaned_text` key removed at any depth — plus a column revoke of `payload` from `authenticated` and `anon`, every API read given an explicit column list, and both `authenticated`-executable RPCs changed from `returns setof jobs` to an explicit body-free projection. `answer_job_checkpoint` loses its `p_payload` parameter and merges the answer into the Job's own stored payload, validating `choice` against the stored prompt inside the function. `_summarize` no longer truncates a `fetch_page` result into `job_events.detail`; the tool declares a length-and-hash summary instead. Closes `.claude/plans/cleaned-text-exposure.md` and `.claude/plans/checkpoint-payload-injection.md` (the RPC route). | Three things were settled the non-obvious way. **Read side, not write side**: two earlier revisions proposed stripping the text on write and rehydrating on resume, and every blocker traced to one cause — resume starts *past* FETCH, the canonical copy does not exist for a Job parked at its first checkpoint, and an emptied Source is **skipped** by `extract.py` rather than raising. Silent wrong results are worse than the exposure; making the text unreadable touches the worker not at all. **A generated column, not a definer view or a worker-side strip**: the worker is not the only writer of `jobs.payload`, so a sanitization it performs is bypassable, and a definer view would be a second copy of the RLS predicate to drift. **The revoke alone is not the control**: column ACLs apply to table references, not to composite rows returned by a function, so an RPC returning `setof jobs` hands the bodies straight back — and the same revoke, composed with a replace-the-payload RPC, would have turned every checkpoint answer into silent data loss. That last one is why the injection finding and the disclosure finding had to land together. The residual — present-but-unreadable rather than absent — is recorded as R14 with three standing guards, because a blanket `grant select on all tables` is how this exact control was undone twice before. | §16, §17, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `.claude/plans/cleaned-text-exposure.md`, `.claude/plans/checkpoint-payload-injection.md` |
| 2026-08-06 | **v3.57: DM-9 lands, and a Replay Capture's clock is staged while everything else stays real.** `manzil export-demo-capture <job_id>` records a finished ingest — the `job_events` timeline, the real per-stage durations, the accrued cost and the finished Listing — and the browser replays it into the query cache when a Demo Account submits a URL. The Tasks UI is driven **unchanged**: `JobCard` and `PipelineTrack` read their ordinary query keys and cannot tell the difference. (This entry originally also had the replay park at a recorded checkpoint and resume when the visitor answered it; **v3.59 cut that** — see below.) Maps are switched off at the `MapFrame` seam, so `loadGoogleMaps()` is never called in a demo session. The one deliberate infidelity: playback is compressed from three-to-five minutes to about thirty-five seconds, scaled proportionally with a per-gap floor and seeded jitter, and the disclosure states the real duration and the speed-up before the visitor confirms. | Nobody watches a demo for five minutes, so a faithful clock would mean nobody sees the pipeline at all — but §3 pins a Replay Capture as *a recording, never a simulation*, and quietly shortening it would erode exactly the claim that makes replaying honest. Naming the compression, keeping the recorded durations in the bundle, and disclosing both is what lets the demo be quick and still be truthful. The pacing is proportional rather than uniform because a replay where every stage takes the same beat reads as a progress bar rather than as work; it is jittery because a metronome is subtly wrong in a way people notice without being able to say why; and the checkpoint wait is excluded from the budget because a decision the visitor controls is not ours to schedule. Replaying in the browser rather than through the worker's replay mode is what keeps "a demo writes nothing" literally true: no Job, no `job_events`, no `job_stage_costs`, because nothing is ever sent anywhere. | §3, §10, §12, §13, §16, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `worker/.../export_demo_capture.py`, `frontend/src/features/demo/replay/` |
| 2026-08-06 | **v3.56: demo identity fails closed, disabling revokes, public issuance is bounded, and private images require a Hunt context for everyone.** Answers `docs/demo-mode-security-impl-reanalysis.md`. **C1**: every control asked `is_demo_account(auth.uid())`, and both read predicates were written `not is_demo_account(...) or <scoping>` — so deleting the marker row promoted a live demo token to an ordinary authenticated reader of every Property, Source and image in the project, with `demo_enabled` bypassed. Identity is now `private.demo_identity()` — `none | scoped | dark` — keyed on the signed `manzil_demo` claim **or** the marker, where reaching `scoped` additionally needs the singleton, the switch and a matching generation. **C2**: `issue_demo_session` counted, decided and inserted with no lock, so concurrent callers all passed the ceiling; and every refused request, including while the demo was off, appended a durable row. It now decides under an advisory transaction lock and records into a fixed-size bucketed counter table. **H2**: `site_settings.demo_generation` rotates on every toggle and is minted into each token, so disable-then-enable is a revocation rather than a pause. **H5**: `private.can_read_property_image()` enforces §8.3's Hunt context for every authenticated subject, not only the demo principal. Plus H1 (the seed reconciles the stale singleton *before* inserting, so an installation predating v3.55 can actually be upgraded), H3 (TTL bounded 60s–30min at startup), H4 (enable is atomic with its audit entry; disable is deliberately not), M1–M7. | Three of these are the same mistake in different clothes: a security decision that depended on some *other* row still being correct. A marker row that must exist, a count that must not race, a token that must not outlive the configuration that authorised it. The fix in each case was to make the failing direction the closed one — which is why identity keys on a claim the attacker cannot remove rather than on a row an operator can. The audit asymmetry is the one deliberate inconsistency: making the app publicly reachable with no record of who did it is unacceptable, so enable is atomic; a shutoff that a broken ledger can refuse is *worse* than an unlogged shutoff, so disable is not. Storage was ruled the other way from how the code had drifted — DESIGN §8.3 has always required a Hunt context, images disclose more than the structured facts do, and the demo predicate turned out to need no special case once authorization routed through the already-demo-aware `member_role()`. | §8.3, §16, §17, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `docs/demo-mode-security-impl-reanalysis.md`, `.claude/plans/demo-mode-hardening.md` |
| 2026-08-05 | **v3.55: the Demo Account becomes a virtual principal — a subject with no `auth.users` row.** Closes the last release blocker: a shared GoTrue identity is an account-takeover and denial-of-service primitive however read-only the database is. The demo principal is now a bare UUID in `demo_accounts` (the FK to `auth.users` is dropped); `POST /v1/demo/session` mints a short-lived HS256 token for it, `get_current_user` verifies that token locally, and its Curator role is **synthesised** by `private.member_role()` rather than stored, since a subject with no account cannot hold a `hunt_members` row. The membership guard accordingly refuses *every* stored membership for a demo principal, and the preflight asserts both that no `auth.users` row exists and that no membership does. | Settled by probing a running stack rather than by reasoning, because the obvious answers are wrong. **A self-minted, sessionless token did not help:** GoTrue honoured `PUT /auth/v1/user` and changed the password (200). **Banning the account did not help either:** still 200 — a ban blocks password sign-in (`400 user_banned`) and nothing else, because GoTrue trusts any validly-signed JWT for `/user` operations. Only the third shape works: with no `auth.users` row, PostgREST serves the token normally while GoTrue returns `403 user_not_found` for `/user`, `updateUser`, `logout` and `reauthenticate` alike. That is a structural guarantee rather than a configuration one — it cannot drift when a provider default changes, because there is nothing for the provider to act on. The visible cost is that the demo visitor is absent from the member list and from Presence, which is the honest rendering anyway: they are not a person in the Hunt. | §3, §16, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `docs/demo-mode-security-impl-analysis.md` |
| 2026-08-05 | **v3.54: the demo kill switch reaches Hunt-scoped data, private Storage is scoped, and the money-spending RPCs guard at entry.** Answers the three release-blocking findings in `docs/demo-mode-security-impl-analysis.md`. `private.member_role()` becomes demo-aware, so hunts, listings, comments, jobs, scores and every `visit_*` table inherit both the Demo-Hunt scoping and `demo_enabled`; the `property-images` Storage policy takes the same Property predicate its metadata row does; and `submit_listing`, `answer_job_checkpoint`, `set_listing_source_policy` and `correct_auto_resolved_checkpoint` each reject a demo subject at function entry. Also: the demo role is Curator exactly (not `member`), `demo_accounts` is a real singleton, `demo_enabled` cannot be true without a Demo Hunt, demo metadata is exposed only through `public.demo_context()`, and `private.demo_preflight()` gates enablement. | Two of these were gaps between what DESIGN claimed and what the code did, which is the failure mode this project's stop-and-flag rule exists to catch — §16 said disabling the demo darkens PostgREST while the switch reached only the eight global fact tables, and §16 item 4 promised RPC guards that a migration comment had argued out of existence. The RPC argument was wrong in a specific way worth recording: the statement trigger *does* block their writes today, but a guard that depends on reaching a particular INSERT breaks the moment one of those functions grows a side effect before it, and they are the cost-bearing entry points. Fixing the kill switch at `member_role()` rather than by adding restrictive policies to forty tables is the same instinct that put the write guard in one trigger: a boundary spread across forty places is a boundary somebody will forget in the forty-first. Storage was relying on path secrecy, which is a reasonable posture among two trusted accounts and none at all once one of them is the public. | §3, §16, §17, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `docs/demo-mode-security-impl-analysis.md` |
| 2026-08-05 | **v3.53: Demo Mode — the app becomes publicly viewable behind one shared, database-enforced read-only account; and the pre-existing global-table read exposure it revealed is closed for everyone.** A **Demo Account** (`demo_accounts`) is a Curator of one **Demo Hunt** (`site_settings.demo_hunt_id`, owned by the primordial Site Admin, populated with real ingests the Owner pays for normally). Enforcement is DB-first and assumes the JWT is driven straight at PostgREST with the API bypassed: restrictive `SELECT` scoping on the global fact tables, `revoke select (cleaned_text) on property_sources from authenticated`, a statement-level write-refusing trigger on every `public` table, explicit subject-guards inside the four money-spending `security definer` RPCs and the three service-role membership RPCs, and `demo_enabled` wired into RLS so the kill switch darkens PostgREST and Realtime rather than only the API. A demo submission is disclosed and then served by a **Replay Capture** — a recorded `job_events` timeline plus finished payload, replayed in the browser — so no Job is enqueued, no page is fetched, and no row is written. Maps render pre-captured statics; `loadGoogleMaps()` is never called. Demo writes apply to the local query cache and vanish on reload. Owner rulings taken with the change: DESIGN is authoritative over the `cleaned_text` contradiction (the code was wrong), and per-run LLM cost stays visible to demo visitors. | Two independent findings drove the shape. First, **writes were never the exposure**: `property_sources`, `properties`, `extractions` and five sibling tables are `for select to authenticated using (true)` with no column grant anywhere, so any account could already read the cleaned text of every page ever fetched and every address ever researched, across every Hunt — which §16 has claimed since 2026-06-28 was service-role-only. Two trusted accounts is what hid it; handing one to the internet is what would have published it. That fix is therefore a prerequisite and is worth having whether or not the demo ever ships. Second, **the boundary has to be Postgres**, because a demo visitor holds a real session in a browser they control: an API middleware, a frontend interception, and a disabled button are all UX, and the trigger was chosen over amending the write policies precisely because so many of them say `member_role(...) is not null` and would have admitted a demo Curator silently. Replaying in the browser rather than through the worker's existing `MANZIL_LLM_MODE=replay` is what makes "never writes a row" literally true — replay makes the model calls free but a real Job still writes eight tables and would need a purge path. Keeping the Demo Account on the Curator role rather than inventing a fourth role keeps the permission matrix single-sourced and stops a write policy ever deriving authority from it. Cost stays visible because per-run spend is a first-class surface (FR10) and NFR1's <$0.15/listing target is worth showing, not hiding. | §2.4, §3, §4.2, §16, §17 (R2 + new R12), §18, §19, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `.claude/plans/soft-finding-newell.md` |
| 2026-08-09 | **v3.67: the demo's map stills are basemaps, and the pins over them stay live.** DM-9's pre-captured map imagery is captured without markers; both map surfaces draw their real pins over the still from live data, projected with the geometry the still was captured at. The capture records `center`/`zoom`/`size` alongside each image as a contract the frontend depends on, and both colour schemes are captured because the basemap is restyled at runtime. A Property with no still keeps the honest "Map not shown" state rather than falling back to a live load. | Baking pins into the image was the obvious route and is wrong twice. A pin's colour *is* its Unit Group's score band, so a baked pin is a scoring claim frozen into a raster — change the Rubric and the map keeps asserting a band the Hunt no longer computes, the same staleness that already forces a Replay Capture to be re-exported, except silent and in a picture nobody thinks to re-check. And a baked pin cannot be clicked, while the map surface exists precisely to be the way into the drawer; a demo whose pins ignore the pointer teaches a visitor the wrong thing about the product. Keeping the basemap static and the pins live costs one projection routine, duplicated deliberately on both sides of the seam and unit-tested, and buys a map that cannot lie about a score and does not stop working when the Rubric moves. The basemap itself is safe to freeze: streets do not depend on the Rubric. | §13.2, §16, §20; `scripts/capture_demo_maps.py`, `frontend/src/features/map/` |
| 2026-08-09 | **v3.66: the Demo Hunt's second voice is a real person's account, and seeded opinions are no longer treated as demo content.** The collaboration persona the seed script adds so the Demo Hunt has more than one voice was a disposable account (`sam@manzil.local`, "Sam"); it is now the Owner's own account under a per-Hunt display name, and the fabricated comments, ratings and Interest Statuses it carried have been deleted from the live Demo Hunt. Two constraints follow. The persona's real `default_display_name` is never published — the per-Hunt `display_name` override established in v3.55's privacy pass is the only name a visitor sees, and it now covers the persona for the same reason it covers the Owner. And provisioning changes character: creating a GoTrue account on a real personal address is [§4.1](#41-authentication)/PR-1's territory, not a seed script's, so the script looks up the account and only creates as an announced fallback. | A demo shows a collaborative tool, which needs two voices; that was the persona's whole justification. But a second *fake person* buys nothing a second real account does not, while adding an unowned credential to every environment the seed touches — and the canned opinions it existed to author were the part that actually misled, appearing on real Listings in the Owner's own adopted Hunt where they were indistinguishable from real notes. The Owner reading "Sam" on their own data and not recognising the name is the failure mode in miniature. Deleting the fabricated content and keeping only genuine authorship holds [§16](#16-security-and-privacy)'s line that a demo may compress the clock but must not invent the substance — the same rule that makes a Replay Capture a recording rather than a simulation. What remains open is that the generator still exists in the seed script, so an ordinary re-run reinstates the fabrication under a real name; that is recorded in `IMPLEMENTATION.md` rather than fixed here. | §16, §20; `scripts/seed_demo_hunt.py`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-09 | **v3.65: custom Criteria gain a `manual` acquisition — a Criterion the pipeline never touches and a person answers.** `CustomCriterionDef` adds `acquisition` (`extracted` ❘ `manual`, defaulting to `extracted` so every stored definition keeps its meaning), and `RefreshClass` adds an inert `manual` member. A manual definition must carry `requires_tool = null`, `refresh_class = manual`, and no route modifiers; it may be Property- or Floor Plan–scoped and uses the same boolean/number/controlled-enum value schema as any other custom Criterion. Authoring skips routing classification entirely. CUSTOM_MATCH filters manual definitions out before dispatch, and saving one enqueues the free re-score with no cached-evidence backfill. The sole value path is an ordinary Override, whose value the API now validates against the definition's `value_schema` for manual Criteria only. Changing `acquisition` joins description/route/scope/schema as a semantic change that mints a replacement key. The scoring engine is unchanged. | Not everything worth scoring is on the page. Commute-by-feel, a landlord's tone on the phone, what a friend who lives nearby says — these are real decision inputs that no producer can acquire, and forcing them through a text or Maps route would either invent evidence or leave the Criterion permanently unknown. Modelling them as a producer-free acquisition rather than a new tool keeps §10.9 dispatch deterministic and keeps the engine pure: the resolver's existing `override ▸ extraction ▸ unknown` ladder already scores a hand-entered value correctly, so the feature is a contract and UI change with no scoring-semantics change at all. Validation is load-bearing here in a way it is not elsewhere — for an extracted Criterion a bad value is a pipeline bug caught upstream, but for a manual one the Override *is* the input, and an out-of-schema value would silently score as "matched nothing" and be indistinguishable from a considered answer. The inert `manual` refresh class is preferred over borrowing `listing_details` so the definition does not assert a refetch cadence that nothing honours. | §9.2, §10.9, §20; `shared/models.py`, `api/rubric/service.py`, `api/overrides/service.py`, `worker/stages/custom_match.py`, `frontend/src/features/rubric/`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |

| 2026-08-09 | **v3.68: an archived Listing may be permanently erased from one Hunt by its Owner or a non-member Site Admin, without erasing the shared Property.** Archive remains the reversible everyday action. Permanent deletion exists only in the Archived view, has no bulk form, previews grouped impact, and requires the actor to type the exact Property name. The transaction refuses `queued`, `running`, or `waiting_user` Jobs; deletes the Listing and all of its Hunt-local opinion/history, every Visit of that Property in that Hunt, and Hunt-scoped custom-Criterion Extractions for the pair; and retains only a minimal tombstone plus the Ghost View Admin Audit row. Global Property truth — Property, Sources, Floor Plans, images and Catalog Extractions — and every other Hunt's Listing remain. Authorization is a narrow Postgres function with no direct authenticated `hunt_listings` DELETE policy. Site Admin authority is Ghost View only: membership in the Hunt disables the admin path and the assigned Hunt role governs. The Visit pair is now an FK to the Listing pair with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, closing the race where a tour could be inserted while its Listing was being erased. | A Hunt Owner already controls the complete Hunt opinion layer and can archive the association; making permanent cleanup admin-only would force ordinary data lifecycle work through a support operator without protecting any global fact. Owner power is appropriate only because the boundary stops at that Hunt. The archived-only gate, exact-name confirmation, impact preview, active-Job refusal, lack of bulk action, structural Visit ownership, durable tombstone and atomic audit address accident, stale worker writes, direct-PostgREST bypass, cross-Hunt damage and unattributed support action separately rather than treating a frontend dialog as authorization. | header, §3, §4.2, §5.1, §8.2–§8.3, §13.2, §16, §20; `supabase/migrations/20260901000013_permanent_listing_deletion.sql`, `api/listings`, `api/admin`, `frontend/features/listings`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-09 | **v3.69: every long list is paged at 50/100/250, no picker may enumerate the table behind it, and the admin panel's rail collapses behind a Burger on a phone.** Three rules, one theme. (a) The admin tables (People, Hunts, Jobs, Audit, Feedback) and the Overview page their rows through one shared control — client-side slicing over the already-loaded, already-sorted, already-filtered list, with the rows-per-page choice remembered per screen in the browser. On the Overview, the header checkbox now selects **the current page**; the bulk bar still counts selections made on other pages. (b) A control that picks one row out of an installation-wide table asks the server for suggestions after **three characters** and never on open: `GET /v1/admin/hunts/options?q=` (min 3, capped at 20, no roll-ups) replaces `GET /v1/admin/hunts` in both Hunt pickers on the People page. Pickers scoped to one Hunt's own contents stay as they are. (c) The admin rail keeps its two labelled groups and moves into a Drawer below `sm`, matching `AppLayout`. | The dropdown is the load-bearing one: "Add to a Hunt" fetched every Hunt in the installation with five correlated subqueries and a cost lateral **per row**, to render a list nobody can scroll — a cost that grows with the whole product's size and is paid on a click that usually ends in one selection. Putting the floor in the endpoint signature rather than the component is deliberate: the frontend is where the rule gets forgotten, and a 422 cannot be forgotten. Pagination is a different problem wearing similar clothes — the payloads are capped or filtered already, and what a five-thousand-row `<table>` actually costs is layout on every hover, so slicing client-side keeps sort and filter meaning the same thing on page 2 as on page 1 and is honest about not being a network fix. The remaining network exposure is recorded rather than papered over: `/v1/admin/hunts` still returns every Hunt, and a server-side search/limit for that table is follow-up work, not something to fake with a silent cap that hides rows an operator went looking for. The phone rail was the visible defect — eight links wrapping into rows of pills, group labels dropped because they had nowhere to go, a third of the viewport gone before the page started. | §13.1, §13.2, §20; `frontend/src/components/TablePagination.tsx`, `frontend/src/features/admin/`, `api/admin/router.py`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-10 | **v3.70: the admin Hunt table is paged, searched and ordered by the database, and the follow-up v3.69 left open is closed.** `GET /v1/admin/hunts` takes `search`, `limit` (default 50, ≤ 250) and `offset`, and returns `HuntPage` — `{items, total}` — instead of a bare array. `total` counts the whole match rather than the page, because a pager cannot render "of N" or a last-page control without it and a client that must fetch everything to learn N has not been paged at all. Search matches Hunt name **or** owner name, which is why no caller may re-filter the results by label. Ordering stays "most expensive first". | The v3.69 entry recorded this route as the remaining exposure and said a silent cap was not an acceptable substitute; this is the version that does not hide rows. The load-bearing part is the *shape* of the query, not the `limit`: the obvious implementation — five correlated subqueries in the target list, `order by` the cost lateral, `limit` on the end — makes Postgres compute every roll-up for every Hunt in the installation and discard all but fifty, because a Sort node carries an already-evaluated target list. So the page is chosen first on the ordering key alone and the four per-row counts are joined onto the fifty survivors. What remains is one grouped cost aggregate over the matching set, and that is inherent rather than sloppy: "order by spend" cannot be answered without pricing the candidates, and the alternative — an indexable default order like `created_at` — would trade the panel's most useful question for a cheaper plan. Paging also changes one visible behaviour honestly: the search box now debounces and round-trips, so it is no longer instant on a table small enough to have been filtered locally. That is the correct trade at the size this exists for. | §13.1, §20; `api/admin/{router,schemas}.py`, `frontend/src/features/admin/AdminHuntsPage.tsx`, `frontend/src/components/TablePagination.tsx`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-10 | **v3.71: Demo Mode requires a symmetric JWT signing key, and the project must not rotate away from one.** The Demo Account is a virtual principal with no `auth.users` row ([§20](#20-decision-log) v3.55), so no authentication flow can ever produce its token — `POST /v1/demo/session` **mints** one, signing HS256 with the project's shared secret. That makes the shared secret a structural dependency of the feature, not a deployment detail: if the hosted project's current JWT signing key is asymmetric, the API cannot sign at all and Demo Mode stops working entirely. A hosted project may therefore keep an asymmetric key on standby, but **promoting it to current breaks the demo the moment it happens**. Recorded here because nothing in the code says so and the failure is silent. | Asymmetric signing exists so that *verifiers* need no secret capable of minting. That benefit does not reach a service which is itself the signer: whatever key the API holds can forge a token for any subject, so an imported ES256 private key would carry exactly the blast radius of the HS256 secret while changing nothing about the trust model. The only change that would genuinely free the key type is giving the demo principal a real account — which is the arrangement v3.55 removed after banning that account was found not to stop GoTrue honouring `updateUser`, and after a shared identity proved to be a mass-signout surface. The failure mode is what makes this worth a ruling rather than a comment: a missing or wrong secret degrades **identically to a demo that is simply switched off** — `/v1/demo/config` answers `enabled:false` and `/v1/demo/session` answers `404`, both before any database read — so a routine-looking key rotation would present as an intentionally disabled demo rather than as a fault. | §16, §20 v3.55; `api/demo/service.py`, `api/demo/router.py`, `scripts/dm8_adversarial.py` |
| 2026-08-10 | **v3.72: Demo Mode is selected and explicitly published from the Admin panel; the Site Admin must own the Hunt, active truth stays live, and derived assets move behind an atomic release.** A Site Admin first builds an ordinary Hunt and curates it into the desired public state. Admin → Demo Mode lists only that admin's owned Hunts, inventories the content a Demo visitor can read, requires explicit per-Hunt member display names and exact Hunt-name confirmation, then queues a durable release. Active Listings remain live; every archived Listing with a valid completed ingest becomes an ordered protected Replay Capture; mapped Properties receive versioned light/dark Static Maps basemaps. Ordinary edits only mark derived inputs stale: publication is manual through **Publish updates**, never automatic. A complete worker build is promoted in one transaction after re-checking fingerprint, owner, Site Admin grant, and token generation; failures/supersession leave the prior release intact. Disable immediately revokes sessions but retains the selected Hunt and release. Private captures/tables have no client grants, the `demo-assets` bucket has no read policy, and the API serves only current manifest-declared assets to a current Demo token. The legacy seed/export/map scripts cease to be the production workflow. | The old process made environment variables, a reconciliation script, exported JSON, committed map files, and a primordial-owner convention into hidden production state. That was difficult to update and easy to publish inconsistently. An automatic refresh was rejected because archiving/deleting a Listing would silently alter public material and coordinate changes would spend Google quota; the operator needs a privacy review point. A completely frozen Hunt copy was also rejected because it would duplicate the app's RLS-governed graph and make normal Listing/Rubric curation require a second synchronization system. The hybrid preserves live ordinary data while freezing only the two derived surfaces that cannot be generated by an untrusted visitor. Requiring ownership as well as Site Admin status is deliberate consent and least privilege: Ghost View power to support another Hunt is not permission to publish its collaborators' history to the public. | §2.4, §3, §4.2, §13.1, §16, §18, §20; `IMPLEMENTATION.md`, `frontend/UI_DESIGN.md`, `docs/production-deployment-render-supabase.md` |
| 2026-08-10 | **v3.73: every deletion is confirmed against a named list of its subjects, and irreversible ones are typed back.** One shared `ConfirmDeleteModal` backs every delete in the app. It always lists the subjects by name in a scrolling pane; an irreversible delete also requires the subject's own name typed back (one subject) or the fixed phrase `permanently delete all selected <plural noun>` (several), while a reversible removal asks only for the click. Subjects the server would refuse — an account that owns a Hunt, a Listing with an active Job — are listed with the reason and skipped, never silently dropped, and a changed selection clears whatever was typed. The Admin People roster gains page-scoped multi-select so a bulk account deletion has the same confirmation; deletions run sequentially and the refusals stay selected and are named. This supersedes the AD-3 stance that account deletion is *refused rather than confirmed*: the refusal stays exactly as it was (the API still 409s, the panel still disables Delete and offers the transfer), and the confirmation is now what stands in front of the deletes that are actually permitted. | The panel's destructive actions were one click with no second act at all, so the only thing between an operator and an unrecoverable delete was the server's refusal — which by definition does not fire on the deletes that are allowed. Naming the subjects is the load-bearing half: an operator confirming *five accounts* is confirming a count, and a count cannot be checked against intent. Typing the subject back cannot be done by momentum, which is exactly the failure mode a modal alone does not catch. The fixed phrase for a bulk delete rather than every name in turn because typing twelve names is a task people route around; naming the noun and reading the list is the part that has to happen. Skipping-with-a-reason rather than filtering out because a row that vanishes produces *why is nothing happening*, and the reason is the operator's next instruction. Reversible removals are exempt from typing so that typing keeps meaning *this is permanent*. | §13.2, §20; `frontend/UI_DESIGN.md`, `docs/admin-dashboard.md`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-10 | **v3.74: an invitation survives the sign-in it interrupts, and a Demo session satisfies the route guards.** The auth-gated invitation routes round-trip through authentication: `RequireAuth` redirects to `/login?next=<target>`, and the login page, the magic-link callback, the password-reset completion and `/signout` all carry and re-filter that value through one allow-list (`/invite/…`, `/join/…`, same-origin paths only; `//host` is not a path). The login page says *Sign in to accept your invitation* when it is resuming one, and the invite page names the account that is about to join with a way to switch. Separately, `RequireAuth` and `RequireProfile` now treat a Demo session as authenticated. An email invite to an address with **no** account remains un-mailable — Supabase Auth is the transport and `should_create_user` is false by FR13 — so that case is reported honestly as *invite created, email not sent* and every pending invite exposes a copyable link. | The target used to ride in router state, which does not survive a reload or a second tab: an invited person who reloaded the login page was signed in and dropped on the homepage with no way back to the invitation, which reads as a broken invite rather than a lost variable. A query parameter survives both, and it is also the only form that can be filtered — hence one allow-list rather than four call sites deciding for themselves. Naming the joining account matters because an invitation is addressed to one person but accepted by whoever the browser is signed in as; without it a Hunt gains the wrong member silently. The Demo half is a regression the tokenless demo principal introduced: guards that read only GoTrue saw no session, so every protected route bounced a demo visitor to `/login` — including the two invitation pages, whose *not available in the demo* branches were unreachable. The un-mailable invite is left as a refusal rather than papered over: creating the account would breach FR13, and the honest report plus a copyable link is a working path an Owner can take today. | §13.1, §16, §20; `frontend/UI_DESIGN.md`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |
| 2026-08-10 | **v3.75: P3-22 lands with split email ownership — Supabase Auth owns Auth, Manzil owns product mail.** GoTrue continues to render and send account provisioning, recovery, existing-account magic-link/OTP, and security-change messages through its Resend-backed SMTP. Hunt invitations, checkpoints, failed Jobs, changed Listing scores, comments, and ratings are Manzil events sent through Resend HTTPS from a durable private outbox; the two paths share a provider but never a renderer or session contract. A Hunt invitation always sends to its literal address with the bare `/invite/{token}` capability, never a magic link. Checkpoints go to the Listing submitter; failures to submitter + Owner (deduped); a successful Job emits one changed-score summary per Listing to its submitter; comments/ratings go to other Hunt members. Missing preference rows mean checkpoint/failure on and score/comment/rating off; per-Hunt nullable overrides inherit account defaults. Delivery ids are Resend idempotency keys, retries are bounded, signed Svix webhooks are deduped and monotonic, membership/source eligibility is rechecked at send time, and notification failure cannot fail a Job. The `/account/alerts` surface, per-Hunt Your-profile overrides, invite status/resend, and clay Tasks checkpoint count ship together. Push remains deferred. | Auth email and product email have different trust and language. Making an invitation a GoTrue magic link produced the misleading *Your sign in link* subject, could not reach a non-account address under FR13, and made a forwarded product message an account session. A bare Invite token already owns exactly the intended capability — Hunt membership after ordinary authentication — while an app-owned renderer can name the inviter and Hunt. Keeping GoTrue's security mail avoids rebuilding recovery/OTP semantics; giving product mail a transactional outbox avoids the opposite failure, where a transient Resend response is coupled to the user write or a successful Job. Defaults favor action-required operational mail and keep collaboration noise opt-in. | §5, §5.1, §8.2–§8.3, §9.1, §12, §16, §18–§20; `api/email`, `api/notifications`, `supabase/migrations/20260901000015_notifications.sql`, `frontend/features/notifications`, `IMPLEMENTATION.md` |

---

## 21. Open Questions

1. **Public product name** — Manzil is the codename; decide whether it is also the public-facing name before anything user-facing ships (Phase 1).
2. **Agents-mode default?** — whether any agents-mode component graduates into the default path is deliberately unanswerable until the L2 eval report exists; the adoption rule in §10.11 governs.
